  3. 10: D1 = 1, D2 = 1 ⊕ 0 = 1, giving 11.
  4. 11: D1 = 0, D2 = 1 ⊕ 1 = 0, giving 00.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading Q2 ⊕ Q1 as the next value of the whole state rather than of the single bit Q2, which produces the reversed sequence.

Takeaway. Write the four present states in a table, fill in every input, and read the next state from the same row; the sequence is then forced.

DL-U3B-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following flip-flop conversions are correct? (Q is the present state and the conversion must reproduce the target flip-flop's next-state behaviour for every input.)

  1. JK to T: set J = K = T.
  2. D to JK: set J = D and K = D̄.
  3. T to D: set D = T ⊕ Q.
  4. SR to JK: set S = J and R = K.
Show answer & solution

Concept. A conversion is correct when substituting the wiring into the host flip-flop's characteristic equation reproduces the target's equation for all inputs and both present states.

Step by step.

  1. A: Q+ = TQ̄ + T̄Q = T ⊕ Q, the T equation. Correct.
  2. B: K̄ = D, so Q+ = DQ̄ + DQ = D, the D equation. Correct.
  3. C: a D flip-flop gives Q+ = D = T ⊕ Q, the T equation. Correct.
  4. D: S = J, R = K gives Q+ = J + K̄Q, which is not JQ̄ + K̄Q; for example J = 1, K = 1, Q = 0 would give 1 instead of the toggle to 1 from a hold. The correct SR-to-JK wiring is S = JQ̄ and R = KQ.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (C)

Common misconception. Matching input names instead of behaviours, which is what makes option D look plausible. The names of the inputs are irrelevant; the next-state function is everything.

Takeaway. Prove a conversion by algebra or by a truth table over both states and every input, never by the resemblance of the letters.

DL-U3B-Q21NATeasy

Show answer & solution

Concept. With T = 1 the flip-flop obeys Q+ = Q̄, so it complements its state on every clock.

Step by step.

  1. Start at 0. After clocks 1, 2, 3 the states are 1, 0, 1.
  2. The state is 0 after an even number of pulses and 1 after an odd number.
  3. Seven is odd, so Q = 1.

Answer: 1

Common misconception. Counting the initial state as one of the pulses, which reverses the parity and gives 0.

Takeaway. A T flip-flop with T held at 1 is a divide-by-2 counter; the parity of the clock count is the answer.

DL-U3C-Q14MCQeasy

The characteristic equation of a JK flip-flop is:

  1. Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q
  2. Q+ = JQ + K′Q′
  3. Q+ = J + K
  4. Q+ = J ⊕ K
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The characteristic equation gives the next state as a function of the present state and the control inputs.

Step by step.

  1. From the JK excitation behaviour: J = 1, K = 0 sets, J = 0, K = 1 resets, J = K = 0 holds, J = K = 1 toggles.
  2. SET term: J = 1 and Q = 0 → contributes JQ′. RESET term: K = 1 and Q = 1 → contributes K′Q as the retained part.
  3. Combining: Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q.

Answer: Option A.

Common misconception. Option B swaps the complemented literals, which would make J = 1, K = 0 with Q = 0 produce 0 instead of 1. Always test the equation against the four (J, K) cases.

DL-U3C-Q15MCQeasy

The characteristic equation of a T flip-flop is:

  1. Q+ = T + Q
  2. Q+ = T ⊕ Q
  3. Q+ = T′Q
  4. Q+ = TQ
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A T flip-flop toggles when T = 1 and holds when T = 0, which is exactly the exclusive-OR of T and Q.

Step by step.

  1. T = 0 → Q+ = Q; T = 1 → Q+ = Q′.
  2. The truth table is that of T ⊕ Q.
  3. Equivalently Q+ = TQ′ + T′Q.

Answer: Option B.

Common misconception. Option A (T + Q) never toggles from 1 to 0, and option D (TQ) only ever sets. Both fail the T = 1 test.

DL-U3C-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following configurations make the flip-flop output toggle on every clock edge?

  1. A T flip-flop with T = 1.
  2. A JK flip-flop with J = K = 1.
  3. A D flip-flop with D = 1.
  4. An SR flip-flop with S = R = 0.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Toggling means Q+ = Q′ regardless of the present state.

Step by step.

  1. T flip-flop with T = 1: Q+ = 1 ⊕ Q = Q′ — toggles.
  2. JK flip-flop with J = K = 1: Q+ = 1 · Q′ + 0 · Q = Q′ — toggles.
  3. D flip-flop with D = 1: Q+ = 1 always — that is a set, not a toggle.
  4. SR flip-flop with S = R = 0: Q+ = Q — it holds.

Answer: A and B.

Common misconception. A D flip-flop can toggle, but only if D is fed from Q′. A constant D = 1 merely forces the output high after the first edge.

DL-U3C-Q17NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The excitation table lists, for each required state transition, the input combinations that bring it about. More than one combination may be legal because a don't-care is a free choice.

Step by step.

  1. Use Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q with Q = 0, which reduces to Q+ = J.
  2. For Q⁺ = 1 we need J = 1; the value of K is irrelevant because the K′Q term vanishes at Q = 0.
  3. So both (J, K) = (1, 0) and (1, 1) work — two combinations.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Answering 1 by treating (J, K) = (1, 1) as forbidden. It is forbidden only in an SR flip-flop; in a JK flip-flop it means toggle, and from 0 a toggle reaches 1.

DL-U3C-Q18MCQhard

A JK flip-flop must realise the following behaviour: when input X = 0 the state is retained; when X = 1 the state is complemented. Which input expressions achieve this?

  1. J = X, K = X
  2. J = X, K = X′
  3. J = X′, K = X
  4. J = Q, K = Q
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Translate a required next-state function into JK excitation values.

Step by step.

  1. The required function is Q+ = X ⊕ Q = XQ′ + X′Q.
  2. Compare with Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q. Matching coefficients: J = X and K′ = X′, hence K = X.
  3. Check: X = 0 → J = K = 0 → hold. X = 1 → J = K = 1 → toggle. Both requirements met.

Answer: Option A — J = X, K = X.

Common misconception. Option B (J = X, K = X′) gives Q+ = XQ′ + XQ = X, which sets the output to X and ignores the present state entirely.

DL-U3C-Q19MSQmedium

A flip-flop has the next-state table: Q = 0, X = 0 → 0; Q = 0, X = 1 → 1; Q = 1, X = 0 → 1; Q = 1, X = 1 → 0. Which of the following correctly describe it?

  1. Q+ = X ⊕ Q
  2. Q+ = X′Q′ + XQ
  3. It behaves as a T flip-flop with T = X.
  4. Q+ = X + Q
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Read the table, write the minterms, then simplify and compare with known characteristic equations.

Step by step.

  1. Minterms giving Q⁺ = 1 are (Q = 0, X = 1) and (Q = 1, X = 0), i.e. X′Q + XQ′.
  2. That expression is exactly X ⊕ Q — statement A is true.
  3. X ⊕ Q is the T flip-flop characteristic equation with T = X — statement C is true.
  4. Statement B is the XNOR (equivalence) form, which is the complement of the table; statement D is the OR, which would give 1 for Q = 1, X = 1 instead of 0.

Answer: A and C.

Common misconception. Statement B looks like a valid SOP because it has the right shape, but its minterms are the ones where the output is 0.

DL-U3C-Q20MCQhard

A JK flip-flop is wired with J = Q′X and K = QX. Its next-state function is:

  1. Q+ = X
  2. Q+ = X ⊕ Q
  3. Q+ = XQ
  4. Q+ = X′Q′ + XQ
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Substitute the given expressions into the JK characteristic equation and simplify.

Step by step.

  1. Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q = (Q′X)Q′ + (QX)′Q.
  2. (Q′X)Q′ = Q′X.
  3. (QX)′Q = (Q′ + X′)Q = Q′Q + X′Q = X′Q.
  4. Q+ = XQ′ + X′Q = X ⊕ Q.

Answer: Option B — Q+ = X ⊕ Q.

Common misconception. Option D is the XNOR form X′Q′ + XQ, which is the complement of the derived function — it gives 1 exactly where the true next state is 0.

DL-U3D-Q14MCQeasy

A JK flip-flop must make the transition from the present state Q = 0 to the next state Q+ = 1. Which input pair guarantees this transition?

  1. J = 0, K = 1
  2. J = 0, K = 0
  3. J = X, K = 0
  4. J = 1, K = X
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The JK characteristic equation is Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q. With Q = 0 it collapses to Q+ = J, so the next state is whatever J is and K does not matter.

Step by step.

  1. Q = 0 gives Q′ = 1, so Q+ = J.
  2. To obtain Q+ = 1 we need J = 1.
  3. K multiplies Q = 0, so any value of K works: K = X.
  4. Check the other options: J = 0 holds the state at 0, and K = 0 with J = X also leaves the state at 0.

Final answer. J = 1, K = X (option D)

Common trap. Swapping the excitation rows: J = X, K = 1 is the entry for 1 → 0, not 0 → 1.

Takeaway. In the excitation table the set input is active for 0 → 1 and the reset input for 1 → 0; the other input becomes a don’t care.

DL-U3D-Q15MCQeasy

An SR flip-flop obeys Q+ = S + R′Q with the restriction SR = 0. It is in some state and both inputs are held at S = 0 and R = 0. What is Q+?

  1. Q+ = 0
  2. Q+ = Q
  3. Q+ = 1
  4. Q+ is undefined
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. S = 0, R = 0 is the hold combination of an SR element: neither the set nor the reset term is active, so the next state equals the present state.

Step by step.

  1. Substitute: Q+ = 0 + 1 · Q = Q.
  2. The restriction SR = 0 is satisfied, so the equation is valid here.
  3. The state is retained until S or R is asserted.

Final answer. Q+ = Q (option B)

Common trap. Reading R = 0 as “reset asserted” and answering 0. The reset term is R′Q, which is Q when R = 0, so a 0 on R enables the hold.

Takeaway. The undefined case of an SR element is S = R = 1; S = R = 0 is the ordinary memory state.

DL-U3D-Q16MSQmedium

A T flip-flop has its toggle input driven by T = X, where X is an external logic signal. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Q+ = Q ⊕ X.
  2. The flip-flop degenerates into an SR latch, so S = R = 1 must be avoided.
  3. With X held at 1 the output toggles on every active clock edge.
  4. Driving T = Q ⊕ D turns it into a D flip-flop with data input D.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The T flip-flop toggles when T = 1 and holds when T = 0, which is exactly the XOR: Q+ = Q ⊕ T.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: substituting T = X gives Q+ = Q ⊕ X.
  2. (B) is false: a T flip-flop has one input and no illegal combination at all; nothing about it resembles the SR restriction.
  3. (C) is true: Q+ = Q′ when X = 1, so every edge flips the output — a divide-by-two.
  4. (D) is true: Q+ = Q ⊕ (Q ⊕ D) = D, which is the D behaviour.

Final answer. Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common trap. Believing the T input can create an illegal state. Every value of T is legal, which is why T flip-flops are used for dividers and counters.

Takeaway. The T flip-flop is the XOR form of storage, and XOR with a signal is the standard way to convert between flip-flop types.

DL-U3D-Q17MSQeasy

Which of the following excitation-table entries are correct, for a transition from the present state Q to the next state Q+? (Select all that apply.)

  1. JK flip-flop, 0 → 1: J = 1, K = X.
  2. JK flip-flop, 1 → 1: J = X, K = 0.
  3. SR flip-flop, 1 → 0: S = 0, R = 1.
  4. D flip-flop, 1 → 0: D = 1.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The excitation table inverts the characteristic table: it says which inputs produce a required transition. A don’t care appears whenever both values of an input give the wanted next state.

Step by step.

  1. JK 0 → 1: only J = 1 can set the flip-flop, and K cannot matter because Q = 0. So J = 1, K = X.
  2. JK 1 → 1: K must be 0, otherwise J = 1 with K = 1 would toggle to 0. J is free, so J = X, K = 0.
  3. SR 1 → 0: the reset must be asserted, so R = 1, and S = 0 is required to keep SR = 0.
  4. D 1 → 0: the next state equals D, so D must be 0, not 1. Statement (D) is false.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common trap. Filling the don’t cares with 1 everywhere, which produces inputs that fight each other — for example J = K = 1 for 1 → 1.

Takeaway. For a D flip-flop the excitation table is trivial — the input equals the next state — and that triviality is exactly why D flip-flops dominate real designs.

DL-U3D-Q18MCQeasy

A JK flip-flop is wired with J = X and K = X′. What is Q+?

  1. Q+ = X
  2. Q+ = Q
  3. Q+ = X ⊕ Q
  4. Q+ = X′
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Start from Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q and substitute the wired inputs. The complement on K becomes a plain X once K′ is formed.

Step by step.

  1. J = X and K = X′, so K′ = X.
  2. Q+ = XQ′ + XQ = X(Q′ + Q) = X.
  3. Check both values: X = 0 gives J = 0, K = 1, which resets to 0; X = 1 gives J = 1, K = 0, which sets to 1.

Final answer. Q+ = X (option A)

Common trap. Dropping the complement and computing XQ′ + X′Q = X ⊕ Q, which is the answer for a T flip-flop, not this wiring.

Takeaway. Wiring J and K to complementary signals always turns a JK flip-flop into a D flip-flop whose data input is the signal on J.

DL-U3D-Q19MCQhard

Two JK flip-flops hold the state Q1Q2 and are wired with J1 = Q2, K1 = Q2′, J2 = Q1′, K2 = Q1. Starting from Q1Q2 = 00, what is the state after three clock pulses?

  1. Q1Q2 = 00
  2. Q1Q2 = 01
  3. Q1Q2 = 10
  4. Q1Q2 = 11
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both flip-flops are clocked together, so their next states are computed from the present state and applied simultaneously. Each next state follows Q+ = JQ′ + K′Q.

Step by step. Simplify first. For flip-flop 1, J1 = Q2 and K1′ = Q2, so Q1+ = Q2Q1′ + Q2Q1 = Q2. For flip-flop 2, J2 = Q1′ and K2′ = Q1′, so Q2+ = Q1′.

  1. Start 00: Q1+ = Q2 = 0, Q2+ = Q1′ = 1 → 01.
  2. From 01: Q1+ = 1, Q2+ = 0′ = 1 → 11.
  3. From 11: Q1+ = 1, Q2+ = 1′ = 0 → 10.

Final answer. Q1Q2 = 10 (option C)

Common trap. Updating the flip-flops one after the other, so that the new Q1 is used to compute Q2 in the same edge. Both next states use the present state only.

Takeaway. A two-flip-flop circuit is a four-state machine: tabulate all four rows and the cycle length falls out (here 4, through 00 → 01 → 11 → 10).

shift-registers 24
DL-U3-Q04MCQeasy

A 4-bit serial-in serial-out shift register initially holds 0000 and shifts one position to the right on every clock, the incoming bit entering at the most significant end. The serial input sequence 1, 0, 1, 1 is applied, one bit per clock, starting with the first bit. What does the register hold after the fourth clock?

  1. 1011
  2. 1101
  3. 0110
  4. 1110
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A shift register moves its contents one stage per clock. On a right shift the new bit occupies the most significant position and every old bit moves one place towards the least significant end, so the oldest bit is pushed out of the register.

Step by step. Write the register most significant bit on the left and move every bit one place right, then insert the new bit on the left:

  1. Start: 0000.
  2. Clock 1, input 1: 1000.
  3. Clock 2, input 0: 0100.
  4. Clock 3, input 1: 1010.
  5. Clock 4, input 1: 1101.

A fifth clock would give 1110, which is why the count of clocks has to be followed exactly.

Answer: 1101 — option (B).

Common misconception. Reading the four applied bits as a word and answering 1011. After four clocks the register holds the four most recent input bits, but in reverse order, because each new bit enters at the left while the older ones move right.

Takeaway. Always draw the four-stage box and move bits physically; a four-clock trace costs ten seconds and removes every direction ambiguity.

DL-U3-Q05NATeasy

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Johnson (twisted-ring) counter shifts a single run of 1s in and a run of 0s out, and inverts the feedback. Its cycle length is 2n for n stages, twice the ring counter's n.

Step by step.

  1. Start from 0000 with the inverted output 1 entering at the left.
  2. The states are 0000, 1000, 1100, 1110, 1111, 0111, 0011, 0001, and the next clock returns to 0000.
  3. Count them: eight states, which is 2n = 2 × 4 = 8.
  4. The counter therefore divides the clock by 8 and leaves 16 − 8 = 8 states unused.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Answering 4, which is the ring counter's cycle length, or answering 16, which is the number of states the four flip-flops can hold rather than the number the cycle visits.

Takeaway. Memorise the pair: n stages give a ring counter of modulus n and a Johnson counter of modulus 2n.

DL-U3-Q17MCQmedium

A 4-bit ring counter is initialised to 1000 and shifts one position to the right on every clock, with the last stage fed back into the first. What does it hold after three clock pulses?

  1. 0100
  2. 0010
  3. 1000
  4. 0001
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A ring counter is a shift register with the last stage fed back to the first, so the single 1 circulates and never leaves the register.

Step by step.

  1. Start: 1000.
  2. After 1 clock: 0100.
  3. After 2 clocks: 0010.
  4. After 3 clocks: 0001.
  5. A fourth clock returns the counter to 1000, so the modulus is 4.

Answer: 0001 — option (D).

Common misconception. Counting the initial state as one of the three shifts and stopping at 0010. The question counts clock pulses, not states visited.

Takeaway. With a ring counter of n stages the state after k clocks has its 1 at position k mod n.

DL-U3-Q18NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A left shift moves every bit one place towards the most significant end, which multiplies the stored value by 2 and discards any bit shifted out of the top. The vacated least significant position takes the serial input.

Step by step.

  1. Start: 1011 = 11.
  2. After 1 clock: 0110 = 6. The top bit 1 was shifted out.
  3. After 2 clocks: 1100 = 12.
  4. Check by arithmetic: (11 × 4) mod 16 = 44 mod 16 = 12.

Answer: 12

Common misconception. Expecting 44, that is 11 × 4, and forgetting that a 4-bit register can hold only 16 values, so the bits shifted past the top are lost rather than retained.

Takeaway. A left shift is multiplication by 2 modulo the register width; a right shift is division by 2, rounding down.

DL-U3-Q19MSQeasy

Which of the following are standard uses or properties of a shift register?

  1. Converting a serial bit stream into a parallel word.
  2. Delaying a serial data stream by a fixed number of clock periods.
  3. Multiplying an unsigned binary number by 2 by shifting the bits left when no bit is lost.
  4. A 4-bit ring counter built from a shift register has 8 distinct states in its cycle.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A shift register is a chain of flip-flops with a common clock; it converts between serial and parallel form, stores, delays and shifts data.

Step by step — evaluate each option.

  1. (A) True. Load the bits one per clock and read all the outputs together — that is a serial-in parallel-out register.
  2. (B) True. Each stage adds one clock of delay, so an n-stage register delays a serial stream by n periods.
  3. (C) True. A left shift multiplies by 2, provided no significant bit is shifted past the most significant end.
  4. (D) False. A ring counter of 4 stages has 4 states; the counter with 2n = 8 states is the Johnson counter, which inverts the feedback.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating ring and Johnson counters as the same circuit. The ring counter feeds the last stage back directly; the Johnson counter feeds back its complement, which doubles the cycle length.

Takeaway. Ring = n states, Johnson = 2n states; the feedback connection is the whole difference.

DL-U3-Q24MCQhard

A 4-bit ring counter and a 4-bit Johnson counter are each built from a 4-bit shift register. Counting only the states that are not on the main cycle, how many unused states does each counter have?

  1. 12 for the ring counter and 12 for the Johnson counter
  2. 12 for the ring counter and 8 for the Johnson counter
  3. 8 for the ring counter and 8 for the Johnson counter
  4. 4 for the ring counter and 8 for the Johnson counter
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Four flip-flops give 2⁴ = 16 possible states. The number of unused states is 16 minus the length of the counter's main cycle.

Step by step.

  1. Ring counter: the single 1 circulates through 4 positions, so the cycle length is n = 4.
  2. Unused states for the ring counter: 16 − 4 = 12.
  3. Johnson counter: the run of 1s grows and then shrinks, so the cycle length is 2n = 8.
  4. Unused states for the Johnson counter: 16 − 8 = 8.

Answer: 12 and 8 — option (B).

Common misconception. Answering 4 and 8, which are the cycle lengths rather than the unused counts, or assuming both counters waste the same number of states.

Takeaway. The Johnson counter is the one to prefer when state utilisation matters, precisely because it halves the number of unused states.

DL-U3B-Q11MCQeasy

A 4-bit serial-in, serial-out shift register holds 1101. On every clock it shifts one position to the right and the incoming serial bit enters the leftmost stage. The serial input is held at 0. What does the register hold after two clock pulses?

  1. 0110
  2. 1001
  3. 1101
  4. 0011
Show answer & solution

Concept. A right shift moves every bit one stage towards the least significant end, discards the old LSB and fills the vacated MSB with the serial input.

Step by step.

  1. Start: 1101.
  2. First clock: the bits move right, the old LSB 1 is lost, 0 enters at the left, giving 0110.
  3. Second clock: again a right shift with 0 entering, giving 0011.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Shifting towards the left because the serial input is described as entering the leftmost stage; the input fills the hole the shift creates, it does not set the direction.

Takeaway. Fix the direction first, then let the serial input fill only the stage the shift empties.

DL-U3B-Q12MCQeasy

A 3-bit Johnson counter is built from a 3-bit shift register whose last stage, complemented, is fed back into the first stage. How many of its 8 possible states lie outside the main cycle?

  1. 0
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Concept. An n-bit Johnson counter has a main cycle of 2n states, so the unused states number 2n − 2n.

Step by step.

  1. Here n = 3, so the register has 23 = 8 states.
  2. The main cycle holds 2 × 3 = 6 states: 100, 110, 111, 011, 001, 000.
  3. The remaining states 010 and 101 form a second two-state cycle, so 2 states are outside the main cycle.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 0 because “a Johnson counter uses all the states”. It uses 2n of the 2n states; only a full binary counter uses them all.

Takeaway. For an n-bit Johnson counter the main cycle is 2n states and the leftovers are 2n − 2n.

DL-U3B-Q13MCQeasy

A 4-bit register holds 1011. It is shifted left by one position, with 0 entering at the least significant end. What does it hold afterwards?

  1. 0101
  2. 1011
  3. 0110
  4. 1111
Show answer & solution

Concept. A left shift moves every bit one stage towards the most significant end, discards the old MSB and fills the vacated LSB with the incoming bit.

Step by step.

  1. The MSB 1 is shifted out and lost.
  2. The remaining three bits 011 move one place up, becoming 011_.
  3. The 0 fills the LSB, giving 0110.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Writing 10110 and then truncating the wrong end. The bit that leaves is the most significant one, because that is the direction of travel.

Takeaway. A left shift multiplies the unsigned value by 2 modulo 2n: 11 × 2 = 22 = 101102, truncated to four bits = 0110.

DL-U3B-Q14MCQhard

A 4-stage linear feedback shift register is built with a primitive feedback polynomial, so that it is maximal-length. Started from a non-zero state, how many distinct states does it pass through before returning to the starting state?

  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 15
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Concept. An LFSR is a shift register with an exclusive-or of selected stages fed back, and a maximal-length (primitive) configuration cycles through every non-zero state exactly once.

Step by step.

  1. The all-zero state is absorbing: exclusive-or of zeros is zero, so the register would stay at 0 forever.
  2. The remaining states number 24 − 1 = 15.
  3. A primitive polynomial makes those 15 states a single cycle, so the period is 15 clock pulses.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Answering 16, the total number of states, which would require the zero state to be part of the cycle. A non-primitive polynomial gives a shorter period, not a longer one.

Takeaway. Maximal LFSR period is 2n − 1, and the missing state is always the all-zero lock-up.

DL-U3B-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about ring and Johnson counters are TRUE?

  1. An n-bit ring counter has n states in its main cycle.
  2. An n-bit ring counter leaves 2n − n states outside its main cycle.
  3. A Johnson counter is self-starting from any initial state.
  4. An n-bit Johnson counter has 2n states in its main cycle.
Show answer & solution

Concept. Both counters are shift registers with feedback; the feedback decides how many of the 2n states the main cycle can use.

Step by step.

  1. A ring counter feeds the last stage straight back, so a single 1 circulates: exactly n states. A is true.
  2. The remaining 2n − n states are not on that cycle, so B is true.
  3. A Johnson counter feeds back the complement of the last stage, giving 2n states, so D is true.
  4. All-zero is a fixed point of the Johnson feedback, so a Johnson counter started at 000…0 never leaves it; C is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Common misconception. Assuming the unused states are harmless. Both counters must be initialised into the main cycle, and a glitch that lands them elsewhere can leave them cycling outside it.

Takeaway. Ring: n states; Johnson: 2n states; neither is self-starting.

DL-U3B-Q20MSQhard

A 4-bit shift register holds Q4Q3Q2Q1. On each clock it shifts right (Q1 ← Q2, Q2 ← Q3, Q3 ← Q4) and the new Q4 is Q4 ⊕ Q1, computed from the values before the shift. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. If Q1 is taken as the serial output, the output sequence repeats every 4 clock pulses.
  2. Started from 1000, the register returns to 1000 after exactly 15 clock pulses.
  3. The all-zero state is a lock-up state: a register holding 0000 never leaves it.
  4. The register visits every non-zero state before repeating.
Show answer & solution

Concept. This is a 4-stage LFSR with feedback Q4 ⊕ Q1. Its cycle structure is found by iterating the next-state rule over the 16 states.

Step by step.

  1. From 0000 the feedback is 0 ⊕ 0 = 0, so the register stays at zero: C is true.
  2. Starting from 1000 and iterating gives 1000 → 0100 → 1010 → 0101 → 1011 → 1101 → 0111 → 0011 → 1001 → 1100 → 0110 → 0010 → 1001… — iterating the rule to a repeat shows the cycle closes after 15 steps, so B is true.
  3. Fifteen distinct non-zero states exist, and the cycle visits 15 of them, so it visits all of them: D is true.
  4. The output sequence is the Q1 column of that 15-state cycle, so its period is 15, not 4: A is false.

Answer: Options (B), (C), (D)

Common misconception. Judging the sequence by the register width. The width fixes how many states are available, while the feedback decides how many are actually visited.

Takeaway. For any feedback shift register, iterate the rule from the given seed until a state repeats; the number of steps is the period, and the zero state must be checked separately.

DL-U3B-Q23NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Concept. An n-bit Johnson counter has a main cycle of 2n states, leaving 2n − 2n unused.

Step by step.

  1. Total states: 25 = 32.
  2. Main cycle: 2 × 5 = 10 states.
  3. Unused: 32 − 10 = 22.

Answer: 22

Common misconception. Using 2n − n, the ring counter formula, which gives 27. The Johnson feedback doubles the main cycle of a ring counter.

Takeaway. Johnson cycle = 2n, ring cycle = n; subtract the right one from 2n.

DL-U3C-Q21MCQeasy

A 4-bit serial-in, serial-out shift register is loaded with a 4-bit word. How many clock pulses are needed before the first bit appears at the output?

  1. 1
  2. 4
  3. 8
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a serial-in, serial-out register each bit advances one stage per clock, so a bit entering stage 0 reaches the output after as many clocks as there are stages.

Step by step.

  1. The first bit must traverse stages 0 → 1 → 2 → 3, i.e. 4 stage-to-stage transfers.
  2. Each transfer takes one clock pulse.
  3. Hence 4 clock pulses for the first bit, and 4 more to flush the remaining three bits out.

Answer: Option B — 4 pulses.

Common misconception. Answering 8 confuses 'time to first output bit' with 'total time to empty the register'. The question asks for the first bit.

DL-U3C-Q22MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about shift-register loading and unloading are TRUE?

  1. Loading an n-bit word into a serial-in register takes n clock pulses.
  2. A serial-in, serial-out register of n stages needs 2n clock pulses in all — n to load the word and n more to shift it out.
  3. A ring counter is a shift register whose serial input is the complement of its last stage.
  4. A parallel-in, parallel-out register loads a whole word in one clock pulse.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Serial access costs one clock per bit; parallel access costs one clock per word. The register's width fixes both numbers.

Step by step.

  1. Statement A: each clock admits exactly one new bit, so n bits need n clocks.
  2. Statement B: the first bit still has n − 1 stages to cross before it reaches the output, and the remaining bits follow one per clock, so emptying takes another n clocks — 2n in total.
  3. Statement C is false: feeding back the *complement* of the last stage makes a Johnson counter. A ring counter feeds the last stage back unchanged.
  4. Statement D: with a parallel load path every stage is loaded from its own input on the same edge, so one clock suffices.

Answer: A, B and D.

Common misconception. Statement C is the trap — it swaps the two feedback rules. Ring: direct feedback, n states. Johnson: complemented feedback, 2n states.

DL-U3C-Q23NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An n-stage Johnson counter has a cycle length of 2n.

Step by step.

  1. The feedback is the complement of the last stage, so the register first fills with 1s and then with 0s.
  2. The register fills with 1s one stage at a time (6 states), then empties back to 0s one stage at a time (6 more), and the next clock returns it to all zeros.
  3. That is 12 distinct states in the cycle, matching 2n = 2 × 6 = 12.

Answer: 12

Common misconception. Using 2ⁿ = 32 describes the number of possible register contents, not the cycle length. Using n = 6 describes a ring counter.

DL-U3C-Q24MCQmedium

A 4-bit ring counter is initialised to 1000, with the last stage fed back to the first. After 3 clock pulses the register content is:

  1. 1000
  2. 0100
  3. 0001
  4. 0010
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A ring counter rotates a single 1 around the register, one position per clock.

Step by step.

  1. Start: 1000.
  2. After 1 pulse: 0100.
  3. After 2 pulses: 0010.
  4. After 3 pulses: 0001.

Answer: Option C — 0001.

Common misconception. Counting the initial state as 'pulse 1' gives 0010. The initial content is the state at zero pulses.

DL-U3C-Q25MCQhard

A 4-bit shift register holds b3 b2 b1 b0 = 1 0 1 1. It shifts right on every clock, and the serial input is b1 ⊕ b0 of the current content. After two clock pulses the register content is:

  1. 1010
  2. 0101
  3. 1101
  4. 0110
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Trace a feedback shift register one clock at a time; recompute the feedback from the *current* content before each shift.

Step by step.

  1. Initial: 1011. Feedback = b1 ⊕ b0 = 1 ⊕ 1 = 0. After shift 1: 0 1 0 1.
  2. Now b1 = 0, b0 = 1, so feedback = 0 ⊕ 1 = 1. After shift 2: 1 0 1 0.

Answer: Option A — 1010.

Common misconception. Reusing the first feedback value for both shifts gives 0101. The feedback is a function of the register content and must be recomputed every clock.

DL-U3D-Q20MCQeasy

A 4-bit serial-in parallel-out register shifts its contents one position to the right on every clock, with the incoming serial bit entering the leftmost stage. It starts at 0000 and the input bits 1, 0, 1, 1 arrive on four successive clocks. What does the register hold after the fourth clock?

  1. 1101
  2. 1011
  3. 0110
  4. 1000
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a right-shifting register the newest bit occupies the leftmost stage and each earlier bit moves one place to the right, so the first bit in is the rightmost one after four clocks.

Step by step.

  1. Clock 1, input 1: 1000.
  2. Clock 2, input 0: 0100.
  3. Clock 3, input 1: 1010.
  4. Clock 4, input 1: 1101.

Final answer. 1101 (option A)

Common trap. Writing the word in the order the bits arrived, 1011, which is what a left-shifting register would produce.

Takeaway. Fix the shift direction first: in a right shift the first bit received ends up at the least significant end.

DL-U3D-Q21NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A right shift by one position divides an unsigned binary number by 2 and discards the bit that falls off the right end, so three right shifts divide by 23 = 8 with truncation.

Step by step.

  1. 000101102 = 16 + 4 + 2 = 22.
  2. Shift 1: 00001011 = 11. Shift 2: 00000101 = 5. Shift 3: 00000010 = 2.
  3. Equivalently ⌊22/8⌋ = 2, and the three discarded bits were 1, 1, 0.

Final answer. 2

Common trap. Rounding instead of truncating, or applying the shifts as a left shift, which would give 176.

Takeaway. Right shift is integer division by a power of two; the bits that leave the register are the remainder.

DL-U3D-Q22MCQeasy

A 4-bit universal shift register is controlled by two mode inputs S1S0: 00 = hold, 01 = shift right, 10 = shift left, 11 = parallel load. Which mode setting loads all four bits at once?

  1. S1S0 = 00
  2. S1S0 = 01
  3. S1S0 = 10
  4. S1S0 = 11
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two mode bits select, for every stage, between the stored bit, the neighbour on one side, the neighbour on the other side, and the external parallel input. That four-way choice is exactly what a 4-to-1 multiplexer per stage provides.

Step by step.

  1. 00: each stage reloads its own output — hold.
  2. 01 and 10: each stage takes a neighbour — shift right or left.
  3. 11: each stage takes the external bit Di — parallel load, which is the only mode that writes all four stages from outside in one clock.

Final answer. S1S0 = 11 (option D)

Common trap. Confusing the shift-left and shift-right codes, or assuming the highest code must mean “do nothing”.

Takeaway. A universal shift register is four multiplexers plus four flip-flops; the mode word is the multiplexer select word.

DL-U3D-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about a 4-bit universal shift register with hold, shift-right, shift-left and parallel-load modes are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It performs serial-in serial-out, serial-in parallel-out, parallel-in serial-out and parallel-in parallel-out transfer.
  2. A parallel load and a shift cannot both happen on the same clock edge.
  3. It counts in binary with no external logic, because a left shift doubles the value.
  4. In either shift mode exactly one new bit enters and exactly one bit leaves on each clock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four modes are mutually exclusive because the mode word selects one path per stage. Each shift moves the whole word by one position, so exactly one bit crosses each boundary of the register per clock.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the parallel ports and the serial ports can be combined freely, which is why the device is called universal.
  2. (B) is true: the mode word is a single selection, so one edge performs one operation.
  3. (C) is false: shifting doubles the value only until a bit is shifted out; a counter needs an adder or feedback logic to restore the lost bit, and a plain shift register stops counting once the word overflows.
  4. (D) is true by definition of a shift.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common trap. Believing a shift register is a counter. It only counts while the value stays inside the register width and the shifted-in bit is the right one.

Takeaway. A shift register moves bits; it does not compute. Any arithmetic it appears to perform comes from the feedback or the surrounding logic.

DL-U3D-Q24MCQhard

In an n-bit Johnson (twisted-ring) counter, every state other than all-zeros and all-ones can be decoded by a single 2-input AND gate. Why?

  1. Every state has exactly one stage at 1, so one AND gate per stage is enough.
  2. In every such state the register holds a contiguous run of 1s followed by a contiguous run of 0s, so some adjacent pair of stages is complementary.
  3. Adjacent states differ in exactly one bit, so a single XOR detects each state.
  4. The counter is self-starting, so no decoding logic is needed at all.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Johnson counter feeds back the complement of the last stage, so the register content sweeps through the 2n patterns in which the 1s form one contiguous block. Every such pattern has exactly one 1 → 0 boundary, and that boundary is a complementary pair of adjacent stages.

Step by step.

  1. For n = 4 the states are 0000, 1000, 1100, 1110, 1111, 0111, 0011, 0001.
  2. Take 1100: stages 2 and 3 are 1 and 0, so Q2∧Q3′ is 1 for this state and 0 for every other state that has its boundary elsewhere.
  3. All-zeros and all-ones have no boundary at all, which is why they need an n-input gate (or a NOR / AND of all stages) instead of a 2-input one.

Final answer. The contiguous-block structure gives a complementary adjacent pair (option B)

Common trap. Importing a property of a different counter: one-hot decoding belongs to the ring counter, and one-bit adjacency belongs to a Gray-code counter.

Takeaway. Cheap decoding is a property of the state coding, not of the counter’s speed; the Johnson code is chosen precisely so that a 2-input gate suffices.

DL-U3D-Q25NAThard

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each right shift halves the value with truncation, each left shift doubles it, and bits that leave the register are lost for good. So the two directions do not cancel.

Step by step.

  1. 100101102 = 128 + 16 + 4 + 2 = 150.
  2. Right shift 1: 01001011 = 75. Right shift 2: 00100101 = 37.
  3. Left shift 1: 01001010 = 74. Left shift 2: 10010100 = 148.
  4. Check the arithmetic: ⌊150/4⌋ = 37 and 37 × 4 = 148, with no overflow because 148 < 256.

Final answer. 148

Common trap. Assuming the shifts undo each other and answering 150. The two low-order bits 10 were shifted out on the right and never came back; the two zeros shifted in on the left came back as zeros on the right.

Takeaway. A shift register is not a rotation: whatever leaves the end is gone unless it is fed back deliberately, which is exactly what a ring counter does.

setup-and-hold-times 25
DL-U3-Q07MCQeasy

The setup time of a flip-flop is the minimum time for which the data input must be:

  1. held unchanged after the active clock edge
  2. held unchanged before the active clock edge
  3. held unchanged for the whole clock period, on both sides of the edge
  4. kept at logic 1 before the active clock edge so that the input can be captured
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A flip-flop needs its input stable for a short window around the active edge. The part before the edge is the setup time tsu, the part after it is the hold time th.

Step by step.

  1. Inside the flip-flop the input drives a transmission gate and a latch; charge takes a finite time to establish.
  2. If the input is still moving when the edge arrives, the internal node may sit between the two logic levels when the latch closes.
  3. That is why the input must already be settled tsu before the edge — the definition in option (B).
  4. The requirement after the edge is a separate quantity, the hold time.

Answer: held unchanged before the active clock edge — option (B).

Common misconception. Merging setup and hold into a single 'data must be stable around the edge' window. The two are separate constraints with separate fixes: setup is repaired by slowing the clock, hold is not.

Takeaway. Setup looks backwards from the edge, hold looks forwards. Keep the directions straight and the formulas follow.

DL-U3-Q11MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about timing violations is TRUE?

  1. A setup-time violation can be removed by slowing the clock, but a hold-time violation cannot.
  2. Both a setup-time violation and a hold-time violation can be removed by slowing the clock.
  3. A hold-time violation can be removed by slowing the clock, but a setup-time violation cannot.
  4. Neither violation depends on the clock period, because both are properties of the flip-flop alone.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The setup constraint compares the clock period with the path delay, so it contains T; the hold constraint compares the same path delay with a fixed window after the edge, so it does not.

Step by step.

  1. Setup: T ≥ tcq + tcomb + tsu. A larger T — a slower clock — always satisfies it.
  2. Hold: tcq + tcomb ≥ th. No T appears, so changing the clock cannot help.
  3. A hold violation means the data arrives too early and overwrites the value being captured; the remedy is to add delay on the path, not to slow the clock.
  4. Hence only option (A) is correct.

Answer: option (A).

Common misconception. 'Slow the clock down' is offered as a universal fix. It repairs every setup violation and no hold violation, which is why hold problems are usually found only after the design is otherwise complete.

Takeaway. Write both inequalities before answering: whichever one contains T is the one the clock period can fix.

DL-U3-Q12NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a single-cycle path the data launched at one edge must reach the capturing flip-flop and settle before the next edge: T ≥ tcq + tcomb + tsu.

Step by step.

  1. The launch flip-flop needs 2 ns after the edge to present its output.
  2. The logic adds up to 6 ns.
  3. The capture flip-flop needs the data stable 2 ns before the next edge.
  4. So the minimum period is 2 + 6 + 2 = 10 ns.
  5. The highest frequency is 1 / 10 ns = 100 MHz.

Answer: 100 MHz

Common misconception. Dividing by the combinational delay alone, which gives 166.7 MHz and ignores both flip-flop delays. The setup time is as much part of the period as the logic delay is.

Takeaway. The critical-path formula is three terms added, never two: launch delay, logic delay, capture setup.

DL-U3-Q13NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The hold check uses the fastest arrival of new data at the capturing flip-flop: tcq,min + tcomb,min ≥ th. New data that arrives too soon destroys the value being captured.

Step by step.

  1. Earliest arrival of the new value after the edge: 1 + 0.5 = 1.5 ns.
  2. The old value must survive th = 2 ns after the same edge.
  3. Slack = 1.5 − 2 = −0.5 ns, so the path is 0.5 ns too fast.
  4. Adding 0.5 ns of delay brings the arrival to exactly 2 ns, which meets the hold time with zero slack.

Answer: 0.5 ns

Common misconception. Using the maximum delays in a hold check. Hold is decided by the earliest data arrival, so the minimum delays are the ones that matter — the opposite of the setup calculation.

Takeaway. Setup uses worst-case (maximum) delays, hold uses best-case (minimum) delays. Getting that pairing right is most of the marks.

DL-U3-Q14MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about setup time and hold time are TRUE?

  1. Increasing the clock frequency repairs a hold-time violation.
  2. Setup time is measured before the active clock edge.
  3. Hold time is measured after the active clock edge.
  4. A negative hold time means the data may change before the active clock edge and still be captured correctly.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two numbers describe one window around the active edge: tsu before it, th after it. Either may be zero or negative in a real library cell.

Step by step — evaluate each option.

  1. (A) False. The hold constraint tcq,min + tcomb,min ≥ th has no clock period in it, so frequency cannot affect it; a faster clock only makes setup harder.
  2. (B) True. Setup time is the interval before the edge in which the data must already be stable.
  3. (C) True. Hold time is the interval after the edge in which the data must remain stable.
  4. (D) True. A negative hold time means the input may change slightly before the edge and the cell still captures the old value; the window simply extends to the other side of the edge.

Answer: options (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming hold time must be positive. Library cells routinely publish negative hold times, and treating them as an error leads to inserting delay that is not needed.

Takeaway. Picture the window straddling the edge; a negative number simply means the window has slid across the edge, not that the rule has changed.

DL-U3-Q22NAThard

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two independent inequalities must hold. Setup uses the slowest path, hold uses the fastest: T ≥ tcq,max + tcomb,max + tsu and tcq,min + tcomb,min ≥ th.

Step by step.

  1. Hold check: 1.0 + 0.2 = 1.2 ≥ 0.5, so hold is satisfied with 0.7 ns of slack and does not limit the frequency.
  2. Setup check: T ≥ 1.5 + 3.0 + 1.0 = 5.5 ns.
  3. Highest frequency: 1 / 5.5 ns = 181.818… MHz.
  4. Rounded to two decimal places: 181.82 MHz.

Answer: 181.82 MHz

Common misconception. Using the minimum delays in the setup calculation, which gives 1.0 + 0.2 + 1.0 = 2.2 ns and a wildly optimistic 454.55 MHz. Setup is a worst-case check: the slowest launch and the slowest logic decide it.

Takeaway. When a question gives both a minimum and a maximum, it is testing whether you pair them correctly — maximum for setup, minimum for hold.

DL-U3B-Q04MCQeasy

The hold time th of an edge-triggered flip-flop is the minimum time for which the data input must be:

  1. stable before the active clock edge
  2. stable after the active clock edge
  3. held at logic 1
  4. stable for the whole clock period
Show answer & solution

Concept. Setup and hold are the two halves of the sampling window that surrounds the active edge: setup looks backwards from the edge, hold looks forwards.

Step by step.

  1. Setup time guarantees that the data has arrived and settled before the edge.
  2. Hold time guarantees that the data does not change so soon after the edge that the internal latch has not yet captured it.
  3. The two are independent numbers and a flip-flop can have a negative hold time, but neither is a requirement to stay at a particular logic level.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Merging setup and hold into one “data must be stable around the edge” rule. They are checked by different inequalities and, in a path, they are repaired by opposite changes.

Takeaway. Setup is before the edge and is fixed by shortening the path; hold is after the edge and is fixed by lengthening it.

DL-U3B-Q05MCQeasy

A synchronous circuit is timing-clean at 50 MHz, with the same flip-flops and the same combinational logic. Which timing check becomes harder to satisfy as the clock frequency is raised?

  1. The hold check only
  2. Both checks, equally
  3. The setup check only
  4. Neither — frequency changes only the power
Show answer & solution

Concept. The setup inequality contains the clock period, the hold inequality does not. Anything that shortens the period eats setup slack and leaves hold slack untouched.

Step by step.

  1. Setup requires tcq + tcomb,max + tsu ≤ T. Raising the frequency lowers T, so the left side may no longer fit.
  2. Hold requires tcq,min + tcomb,min ≥ th, which contains no T at all.
  3. The hold check is therefore decided by the delays in the path, not by the clock rate.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Assuming that a faster clock stresses every timing rule. Hold violations are frequency-independent; they are usually caused by very short data paths or by clock skew.

Takeaway. Look for T in the inequality: setup has it, hold does not.

DL-U3B-Q06MCQmedium

A path runs from a launching flip-flop, through combinational logic, to a capturing flip-flop. Both are clocked by the same edge with no skew. tcq = 2 ns, the largest combinational delay is 5.5 ns and tsu = 0.5 ns. What is the highest clock frequency at which this path meets setup?

  1. 100 MHz
  2. 125 MHz
  3. 160 MHz
  4. 200 MHz
Show answer & solution

Concept. Data launched at one edge must arrive and settle at least tsu before the next edge, so T ≥ tcq + tcomb,max + tsu.

Step by step.

  1. Sum the delays: 2 + 5.5 + 0.5 = 8 ns.
  2. So the smallest usable period is Tmin = 8 ns.
  3. fmax = 1 / 8 ns = 125 × 106 Hz = 125 MHz.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Dropping tsu because it looks small, which gives 133 MHz, or inverting the sum without converting nanoseconds to seconds.

Takeaway. Frequency is the reciprocal of the summed delay, so keep the units in nanoseconds until the last step.

DL-U3B-Q07MCQhard

In a two-flip-flop path the clock at the capturing flip-flop arrives δ = 0.4 ns later than at the launching flip-flop. Nothing else changes. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. Setup slack improves by 0.4 ns and hold slack worsens by 0.4 ns.
  2. Setup slack worsens by 0.4 ns and hold slack improves by 0.4 ns.
  3. Both slacks improve by 0.4 ns.
  4. Skew affects only the hold check.
Show answer & solution

Concept. Positive skew moves the capturing edge later in time. A later capture is good for setup, because the data has longer to arrive, and bad for hold, because the new data then arrives closer to the capturing edge.

Step by step.

  1. Setup becomes tcq + tcomb,max + tsu ≤ T + δ, so the slack grows by δ.
  2. Hold becomes tcq,min + tcomb,min ≥ th + δ, so the slack shrinks by δ.
  3. Both statements describe the same 0.4 ns, with opposite signs.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Remembering only half the story — that skew helps setup — and concluding that skew is free. Every nanosecond of skew given to setup is taken from hold.

Takeaway. Skew trades setup against hold; only reducing it helps both.

DL-U3B-Q17MSQmedium

In a two-flip-flop path the clock at the capturing flip-flop is delayed by δ > 0 relative to the clock at the launching flip-flop. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. A larger δ relaxes the setup check, because the data has more time to arrive.
  2. A larger δ tightens the hold check, because the capturing edge moves closer to the moment the data changes.
  3. Skew cannot affect the hold check, because hold is measured at the same edge.
  4. Adding a delay buffer in the data path repairs a hold violation but consumes setup slack.
Show answer & solution

Concept. Skew enters both inequalities, with opposite signs. Setup compares the data arrival with the next capturing edge; hold compares it with the same edge.

Step by step.

  1. Setup: tcq + tcomb,max + tsu ≤ T + δ. A larger δ raises the right side, so A is true.
  2. Hold: tcq,min + tcomb,min ≥ th + δ. A larger δ raises the requirement, so B is true and C is false.
  3. A data-path buffer increases tcomb,min and also tcomb,max, so it repairs hold and eats setup slack: D is true.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Common misconception. Believing that hold is immune to skew because it is measured at the launching edge. It is measured at the capturing edge, and skew moves that edge.

Takeaway. Skew is a trade, and the usual repair for hold — extra data delay — is paid for out of setup slack.

DL-U3B-Q22NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Concept. The setup constraint for a single-cycle path with no skew is T ≥ tcq + tcomb,max + tsu.

Step by step.

  1. The data needs tcq + tcomb,max = 1.5 + 6.5 = 8 ns to reach the capturing flip-flop.
  2. It must then stay stable for tsu = 2 ns before the next edge.
  3. So Tmin = 8 + 2 = 10 ns, that is 100 MHz.

Answer: 10

Common misconception. Adding tsu but forgetting tcq, which gives 8.5 ns. The launching flip-flop's own delay is part of the path.

Takeaway. Three terms, one sum: launch delay, logic delay, and the sampling window at the far end.

DL-U3B-Q24NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Concept. Hold slack is the margin by which the earliest possible data change still arrives after the capturing edge has finished sampling: slackh = tcq,min + tcomb,min − th − δ, where δ is the capture-minus-launch clock skew.

Step by step.

  1. Earliest data arrival after the launching edge: 1.2 + 0.5 = 1.7 ns.
  2. The capturing edge is delayed by 0.2 ns, so the data must hold until 0.2 + 1.4 = 1.6 ns.
  3. Slack = 1.7 − 1.6 = 0.1 ns, which is positive, so hold is met.

Answer: 0.1

Common misconception. Using the maximum rather than the minimum delays, or dropping the skew term. Hold is decided by the fastest path, and positive skew makes it worse.

Takeaway. Hold uses minimum delays and subtracts the skew; setup uses maximum delays and adds it.

DL-U3C-Q08MCQeasy

The setup time of a flip-flop is:

  1. The minimum interval for which the data input must be stable before the active clock edge.
  2. The minimum interval for which the data input must be stable after the active clock edge.
  3. The delay from the active clock edge to the change of the output.
  4. The minimum width of the clock pulse.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Setup time tsu is a pre-edge window; hold time th is the post-edge window.

Step by step.

  1. The input is sampled by an internal transmission gate that closes at the clock edge.
  2. The data must already be valid when that gate closes, which gives the window *before* the edge — the setup time.
  3. The data must also stay valid while the gate is closing, which gives the window *after* the edge — the hold time.

Answer: Option A.

Common misconception. Option C is the clock-to-Q delay tcq, a different parameter that appears in the same timing equations.

DL-U3C-Q09MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about setup and hold times are TRUE?

  1. Hold time is measured after the active clock edge.
  2. A setup-time violation can be repaired by reducing the combinational logic delay on the data path.
  3. A hold-time violation can be repaired by reducing the clock frequency.
  4. The clock-to-Q delay of the launching flip-flop contributes to the setup-time constraint.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Setup is a *maximum-delay* (long-path) constraint; hold is a *minimum-delay* (short-path) constraint.

Step by step.

  1. Setup: the data must arrive at least tsu before the next edge, so every delay on the long path hurts. tcq + tcomb,max + tsu ≤ T — statements B and D are true.
  2. Hold: the data must not arrive too early, so short paths hurt. tcq + tcomb,min ≥ th.
  3. The clock period T does not appear in the hold inequality at all, so changing the frequency cannot fix a hold violation — statement C is false.
  4. Hold time is by definition the window after the edge — statement A is true.

Answer: A, B and D.

Common misconception. Statement C is the classic misconception. Slowing the clock lengthens only the period; both the launch and the capture edge move together, so the hold margin is unchanged.

DL-U3C-Q10NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The setup constraint sets the minimum clock period.

Step by step.

  1. The launching flip-flop needs tcq to produce valid data, the combinational cloud needs tcomb,max, and the capturing flip-flop needs tsu of margin before the next edge.
  2. Tmin = tcq + tcomb,max + tsu = 1 + 2 + 1 = 4 ns.
  3. fmax = 1 / Tmin = 1 / (4 × 10-9) = 2.5 × 108 Hz = 250 MHz.

Answer: 250

Common misconception. Forgetting tcq gives 1/3 ns ≈ 333 MHz, and forgetting tsu gives 333 MHz as well — both far too optimistic. All three terms sit on the critical path.

DL-U3C-Q11MCQmedium

A hold-time violation has been detected in a synchronous design. Which remedy can actually remove it?

  1. Increase the clock period.
  2. Insert additional delay in the short data path between the launching and capturing flip-flops.
  3. Reduce the setup time of the flip-flops.
  4. Increase the clock-to-Q delay of the launching flip-flop.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Hold violation is a short-path problem: the data arrives too early and overwrites the captured value.

Step by step.

  1. The hold inequality is tcq + tcomb,min ≥ th, and the clock period does not appear in it.
  2. Therefore stretching the clock period changes nothing — option A is useless.
  3. The only ways to satisfy the inequality are to lengthen the short path (buffer insertion) or to increase tcq / reduce th in the library.
  4. Reducing tsu (option C) helps setup, not hold. Increasing tcq (option D) would also help, but it is a library change, not a design fix — and it worsens setup.

Answer: Option B — add delay to the short path.

Common misconception. Option A is chosen most often. It works for setup violations only. Option D does help hold, but it is not the standard designer's remedy and it costs setup margin.

DL-U3C-Q12NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Hold slack = (arrival time of the earliest data) − (required hold window) = tcq + tcomb,min - th.

Step by step.

  1. Earliest the data can reach the capture point: tcq + tcomb,min = 1 + 0.5 = 1.5 ns after the edge.
  2. The data must not change before th = 0.6 ns after the edge.
  3. Hold slack = 1.5 - 0.6 = 0.9 ns. Positive slack means the circuit is safe.

Answer: 0.9

Common misconception. Using the *maximum* combinational delay instead of the minimum gives a meaningless number. Hold analysis always uses the shortest path.

DL-U3C-Q13MCQhard

A circuit's flip-flops have tsu = 1.0 ns, th = 0.6 ns and tcq = 0.3 ns. The combinational delay between them ranges from 0.2 ns to 4.0 ns, and the skew is 0. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. The circuit is timing-clean at any frequency.
  2. The circuit has a hold violation that cannot be removed by slowing the clock.
  3. The circuit has only a setup violation.
  4. The circuit has both a setup violation and a hold violation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Check both constraints independently; hold does not depend on frequency.

Step by step.

  1. Hold check: tcq + tcomb,min = 0.3 + 0.2 = 0.5 ns, but th = 0.6 ns. Since 0.5 < 0.6, the hold slack is −0.1 ns — a violation.
  2. Setup check: tcq + tcomb,max + tsu = 0.3 + 4.0 + 1.0 = 5.3 ns, so any period ≥ 5.3 ns (≤ 188.7 MHz) is fine.
  3. The hold violation is independent of the period, so it survives at every frequency — statement B.

Answer: Option B.

Common misconception. Seeing a comfortable setup margin, candidates conclude the design is clean. A hold violation can coexist with generous setup slack and will corrupt data at *any* clock rate.

DL-U3D-Q08MCQeasy

A synchronous design meets its setup checks at the target frequency but fails its hold check on one path. Which one of the following changes can remove that hold failure?

  1. Increase the clock period.
  2. Add a delay buffer in the data path between the two flip-flops.
  3. Reduce the setup time of the capturing flip-flop.
  4. Increase the clock frequency.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The hold check compares the earliest possible data arrival against the earliest allowed change after the same clock edge: tcq,min + dcomb,min ≥ th + skew. No clock period appears in it, so changing the period cannot affect a hold result.

Step by step.

  1. Hold is a “too fast” failure: the new data arrives before the capturing flip-flop has finished holding the old one.
  2. The cure is to make the data later, that is to add delay on the path — a buffer chain does exactly that.
  3. Changing the period (A) or the frequency (D) leaves the hold inequality untouched.
  4. Reducing the setup time (C) helps the setup check, not the hold check.

Final answer. Add a delay buffer in the data path (option B)

Common trap. “Slow the clock down” is the reflex answer for every timing failure, but it only ever helps setup.

Takeaway. Setup depends on the period, hold does not; that single sentence classifies almost every timing question.

DL-U3D-Q09NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The setup constraint is T ≥ tcq,max + dcomb,max + tsu + skew. The clock period must cover the launch delay, the logic delay and the capture window.

Step by step.

  1. T ≥ 1.0 + 6.0 + 1.0 = 8.0 ns.
  2. fmax = 1 / 8.0 ns = 0.125 GHz = 125 MHz.
  3. Check: at 125 MHz the data arrives 7.0 ns after the edge and the capture window opens at 7.0 ns, so the setup requirement is met exactly.

Final answer. 125 MHz

Common trap. Forgetting tcq and using 7 ns, which gives 142.9 MHz, or adding th into the setup sum.

Takeaway. Maximum frequency comes from one number, the longest launch-to-capture path, and it is the reciprocal of that path delay.

DL-U3D-Q10MCQmedium

On a flip-flop to flip-flop path, tcq,min = 0.8 ns, the shortest combinational delay is 0.4 ns, the capturing flip-flop has th = 0.5 ns, and the capturing clock arrives 0.3 ns later than the launching clock. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. There is no hold violation; the margin is 0.4 ns.
  2. There is a hold violation of 0.1 ns.
  3. There is no hold violation; the margin is 0.7 ns.
  4. The check cannot be decided without knowing the clock period.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The hold inequality is tcq,min + dcomb,min ≥ th + skew, where a positive skew means the capturing clock arrives later. Later capture is stricter, because the capture edge moves closer to the arriving data.

Step by step.

  1. Earliest data arrival at the capture point: 0.8 + 0.4 = 1.2 ns after the launch edge.
  2. Required hold interval: 0.5 + 0.3 = 0.8 ns.
  3. Margin: 1.2 − 0.8 = 0.4 ns > 0, so the check passes.

Final answer. No violation, margin 0.4 ns (option A)

Common trap. Adding the skew with the wrong sign, which gives 0.1 ns and a phantom violation, or believing the clock period matters — it does not enter the hold inequality.

Takeaway. Positive skew tightens hold and relaxes setup; write the sign convention down before substituting numbers.

DL-U3D-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about setup slack and hold slack are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Lowering the clock frequency removes a hold violation.
  2. Adding delay in the combinational path improves setup slack and reduces hold slack.
  3. Delaying the capture clock relative to the launch clock helps setup and hurts hold.
  4. A hold violation can be repaired by adding buffers on the data path.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Setup slack is T − (tcq,max + dcomb,max + tsu) − skew; hold slack is tcq,min + dcomb,min − th − skew. The two move in opposite directions when the data path or the skew changes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is false: the period does not appear in the hold inequality at all.
  2. (B) is true: more delay eats setup slack but adds hold slack.
  3. (C) is true: a later capture edge gives the data more time to arrive, which helps setup, while the hold window moves later by the same amount, which hurts hold.
  4. (D) is true, and it is the standard fix for hold.

Final answer. Options (B), (C) and (D)

Common trap. Assuming every change must help one check and hurt the other; the clock period is the one knob that touches setup only.

Takeaway. Data delay and skew trade setup against hold; clock period trades nothing, because hold never sees it.

DL-U3D-Q12MCQmedium

A flip-flop to flip-flop path runs with a clock period of 10 ns, no skew, tcq,max = 1.5 ns, tcq,min = 0.8 ns, tsu = 1.0 ns and th = 0.5 ns. Which range of combinational delay keeps both checks satisfied?

  1. 0 ≤ d ≤ 8.5 ns
  2. 0.3 ≤ d ≤ 7.5 ns
  3. 0 ≤ d ≤ 7.5 ns
  4. 0.5 ≤ d ≤ 8.5 ns
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The setup check gives an upper bound on the combinational delay and the hold check gives a lower bound. When the lower bound comes out negative it means no positive delay can violate hold, so the bound collapses to 0.

Step by step.

  1. Setup: d ≤ T − tcq,max − tsu = 10 − 1.5 − 1.0 = 7.5 ns.
  2. Hold: d ≥ th − tcq,min = 0.5 − 0.8 = −0.3 ns.
  3. A negative lower bound is vacuous, since delay cannot be negative; the usable range starts at 0.
  4. Hence 0 ≤ d ≤ 7.5 ns.

Final answer. 0 ≤ d ≤ 7.5 ns (option C)

Common trap. Using tcq,max in the hold inequality, which gives a bogus 0.7 ns floor, or swapping the two bounds.

Takeaway. The maximum delay uses worst-case (slow) launch and setup; the minimum delay uses best-case (fast) launch and hold.

DL-U3D-Q13NAThard

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When launch and capture use opposite edges, the data has only half a period to travel, so the setup inequality becomes T/2 ≥ tcq + dcomb,max + tsu (assuming a 50 % duty cycle).

Step by step.

  1. Time the data needs: 1.5 + 3.0 + 1.0 = 5.5 ns.
  2. T/2 ≥ 5.5 ns, so T ≥ 11 ns.
  3. Equivalently f ≤ 1/11 ns ≈ 90.9 MHz, well below the 181.8 MHz the same path would allow if both flip-flops used the same edge.

Final answer. 11 ns

Common trap. Using the full period and answering 5.5 ns, which ignores that the capture edge is only half a cycle after the launch edge.

Takeaway. Half-cycle paths are the cheapest form of time borrowing, but they halve the budget available to the logic.

Unit 4 — Sequential Circuits: Counters & State Machines · 100 solved questions (2026-10-29)
synchronous-asynchronous-counters 20
DL-U4-Q01MCQeasy

A counter must count from 0 to 59 and then repeat from 0. What is the minimum number of flip-flops required?

  1. 5
  2. 6
  3. 7
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. n flip-flops give 2n distinct binary states, so a modulo-N counter needs the smallest n with 2n ≥ N.

Step by step.

  1. The required states are 0, 1, ..., 59, so N = 60.
  2. Five flip-flops reach 25 − 1 = 31, which is short of 59.
  3. Six flip-flops reach 26 − 1 = 63, and 63 ≥ 59, so six suffice.
  4. Four of the 64 codes are unused; the reset logic must force the counter out of them, which is exactly what the mod-60 decode does.

Answer: 6 flip-flops

Common misconception. Thinking 60 is close to 64 so five bits will do, or dividing 60 by 2 as though each flip-flop stored one decimal digit.

Takeaway. Count the states first, then take ⌈log2 N⌉.

DL-U4-Q02MCQeasy

A 4-bit asynchronous (ripple) up-counter is driven by an 8 MHz clock. The output of the most significant flip-flop is used as a clock further down the system. What is the frequency of that output?

  1. 8 MHz
  2. 4 MHz
  3. 1 MHz
  4. 500 kHz
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a ripple counter each stage is clocked by the output of the stage before it, so each stage divides the frequency by exactly 2.

Step by step.

  1. Q0 is clocked by the 8 MHz source, so it runs at 8/2 = 4 MHz.
  2. Q1 is clocked by Q0 and runs at 2 MHz; Q2 at 1 MHz.
  3. Q3, the most significant bit of a 4-bit counter, is the fourth stage: 8 MHz / 24 = 500 kHz.

Answer: 500 kHz

Common misconception. Assuming every output runs at the clock frequency, or dividing by 4 because the counter has four bits — the division is by 2n, not by n.

Takeaway. An n-stage ripple counter divides by 2n.

DL-U4-Q03MCQeasy

A modulo-13 ripple counter is built from a 4-bit binary counter: a NAND gate decodes one state and drives the asynchronous active-LOW CLEAR input. The counter must display 0, 1, ..., 12 and then return to 0. Which state must the gate decode?

  1. 1100
  2. 1111
  3. 1101
  4. 1001
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An asynchronous CLEAR acts the instant the decoded pattern appears on the outputs, so the gate must decode the first state that is not part of the required sequence.

Step by step.

  1. The required states are 0 to 12, thirteen states.
  2. The first illegal state is 13, which is 1101.
  3. On reaching 1101 the NAND output goes low for a few gate delays, every flip-flop is cleared, and the counter restarts at 0000; the illegal state is too short to be seen.

Answer: 1101

Common misconception. Decoding 1100, the last valid state. Because the clear is immediate, that turns the circuit into a modulo-12 counter which never displays 12. In a synchronous design the load takes effect on the next edge, so there the last valid state is the one to decode.

Takeaway. Asynchronous clear: decode N. Synchronous load: decode N − 1.

DL-U4-Q04MCQmedium

A 4-bit ripple counter is built from negative-edge-triggered flip-flops, each with a clock-to-output delay of 12 ns. What is the highest clock frequency at which all outputs have settled before the next active edge?

  1. 20.83 MHz
  2. 41.67 MHz
  3. 83.33 MHz
  4. 12.00 MHz
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a ripple counter the last stage changes only after a chain of n flip-flop delays, so the clock period must exceed n · tpd.

Step by step.

  1. Four stages in series: 4 × 12 = 48 ns is the settling time of the most significant bit.
  2. The next clock edge must not arrive before that, so Tmin = 48 ns.
  3. fmax = 1 / 48 ns = 20.83 MHz.

Answer: 20.83 MHz

Common misconception. Using a single delay and answering 83.33 MHz. That is the figure for a synchronous counter, where all flip-flops are clocked together and only one delay plus the next-state logic separates the edges.

Takeaway. Ripple period ≈ n · tpd; synchronous period ≈ tpd + tlogic.

DL-U4-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about a synchronous binary counter are TRUE?

  1. Every flip-flop is clocked by the same clock signal.
  2. Its maximum clock frequency is set by one flip-flop delay plus the delay of the next-state logic, not by the number of flip-flops.
  3. In a ripple counter the maximum frequency falls roughly in proportion to the number of stages, which a synchronous counter avoids.
  4. Its outputs settle only after a delay equal to n times the flip-flop delay.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. “Synchronous” means one common clock, so every flip-flop samples at the same instant and only the next-state logic lies between the edge and the settled state.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True by definition; this is what separates a synchronous counter from a ripple counter.
  2. (B) True: the period is tcq + tlogic + tsu, independent of how many bits the counter holds, except that deeper terminal-count logic may add a level or two.
  3. (C) True: a ripple counter needs n · tpd, so its maximum frequency falls as the width grows.
  4. (D) False: n · tpd is the ripple settling time. A synchronous counter settles in one delay plus the logic delay.

Answer: (A), (B), (C)

Common misconception. Treating (D) as a property of all counters; it is the defining weakness of the ripple design.

Takeaway. One clock, one delay, one logic depth — that is what synchronism buys.

DL-U4-Q21NATeasy

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each stage of a ripple counter halves the frequency of the stage before it, so the n-th stage runs at f / 2n.

Step by step.

  1. The most significant bit of a 4-bit counter is the fourth stage.
  2. 1000 Hz / 24 = 1000 / 16.
  3. 1000 / 16 = 62.5 Hz.

Answer: 62.5 Hz

Common misconception. Dividing by 4, once per bit rather than once per power of two, or rounding 62.5 to 63.

Takeaway. The MSB of an n-bit counter toggles once every 2n−1 clocks, which is a frequency of f/2n.

DL-U4-Q22NATmedium

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a ripple chain the first flip-flop divides the source by 2 and the second divides that by 2 again.

Step by step.

  1. The first flip-flop is driven by the 20 MHz source: its output is 10 MHz.
  2. The second flip-flop is driven by the first: its output is 10 / 2 = 5 MHz.
  3. Equivalently 20 / 22 = 5 MHz.

Answer: 5 MHz

Common misconception. Counting the source as stage 1 and reporting 10 MHz, or dividing by the number of stages (2) as though it were a parallel divider.

Takeaway. Count stages from the source: the k-th flip-flop runs at f/2k.

DL-U4-S2-Q01MCQeasy

A 3-bit synchronous binary up-counter is built from three T flip-flops labelled Q2Q1Q0, with Q0 the least significant bit. Which expression must drive the input T2 so that the count advances by one on every clock pulse?

  1. Q0
  2. Q1
  3. Q1Q0
  4. Q2Q1Q0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a synchronous binary counter every flip-flop is clocked at the same instant, so each stage must be told in advance whether it will toggle. Bit i toggles exactly when every lower bit is already 1, because that is the only time a carry reaches it; hence Ti = Qi−1Qi−2 … Q0.

Step by step.

  1. Q0 toggles on every clock, so T0 = 1.
  2. Q1 toggles whenever Q0 is 1, so T1 = Q0.
  3. Q2 is the most significant bit, so it toggles when the count is 011 and about to become 100, that is when Q1 = Q0 = 1.
  4. Check on the transition 011 → 100: with T2 = Q1Q0 = 1, Q2 toggles 0 → 1 while Q1 and Q0 keep their values, giving 100 as required.

Answer: T2 = Q1Q0

Common misconception. Writing T2 = Q1, which makes the counter behave as a ripple counter with the top bit toggling on every other clock. The top bit of an up-counter toggles once every four clocks, not once every two.

Takeaway. In a synchronous counter, stage %s toggles on the AND of all bits below it; in a ripple counter that same AND is supplied implicitly by the carry ripple.

DL-U4-S2-Q02MCQeasy

A 4-bit Johnson counter is driven by a 12 kHz clock. What is the frequency of the output of its last stage?

  1. 1.5 kHz
  2. 3 kHz
  3. 750 Hz
  4. 6 kHz
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Johnson (twisted-ring) counter shifts one bit per clock and feeds back the complement of the last stage, so an n-stage counter passes through 2n states instead of n. Every output therefore completes one cycle in 2n clocks and runs at f / 2n.

Step by step.

  1. Four stages give 2n = 8 states in the main cycle: 0000, 1000, 1100, 1110, 1111, 0111, 0011, 0001.
  2. The last stage is 0 for the first four states and 1 for the next four, so it repeats with period 8.
  3. Divide the clock by 8: 12000 / 8 = 1500 Hz.
  4. That is 1.5 kHz.

Answer: 1.5 kHz

Common misconception. Dividing by 4 because there are four stages, which is the ring-counter rule. A Johnson counter uses 2n states, so the division ratio is 8.

Takeaway. Ring counter: divide by n. Johnson counter: divide by 2n. Count the states before you divide.

DL-U4-S2-Q03MCQeasy

A 4-bit ring counter shifts left on every clock and feeds the last stage back into the first. If it powers up holding 0110, how many distinct states does its cycle contain?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A ring counter circulates its bit pattern unchanged; only the position of each 1 moves. The state therefore returns to the starting pattern after as many clocks as there are bits in the pattern, which need not be the number of stages.

Step by step.

  1. Start: 0110.
  2. Shift left, feeding back the old Q0: 1100.
  3. Again: 1001, then 0011.
  4. One more shift returns %s, so the cycle is 0110 → 1100 → 1001 → 0011 → 0110, four states.

Answer: 4 states

Common misconception. Answering 4 because "there are four stages" happens to be right here, but answering 8 by analogy with a Johnson counter. The correct reasoning is that the two 1s are two positions apart, so the pattern repeats after four shifts. A power-up pattern of 1010 would give a cycle of only 2 states.

Takeaway. In a ring counter the cycle length equals the smallest shift that maps the pattern onto itself, which is the number of stages divided by the number of 1s when the 1s are evenly spaced.

DL-U4-S2-Q04MCQhard

A 3-bit counter is built from T flip-flops with T2 = Q1, T1 = Q0 and T0 = 1. Starting from state 000, how many distinct states does the resulting cycle contain?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 6
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The next state of a T flip-flop is Qi ⊕ Ti. With all three inputs fixed by the present state, the machine is a deterministic 3-bit state machine, so the cycle must be found by following the transitions, not by assuming a binary count.

Step by step.

  1. 000: T = (Q1, Q0, 1) = (0, 0, 1), so Q0 becomes 1 and the state is 001.
  2. 001: T = (0, 1, 1), so the next state is 010.
  3. 010: T = (Q1, Q0, 1) = (1, 0, 1), so Q2 and Q0 toggle while Q1 stays 1, giving 111.
  4. 111: T = (1, 1, 1), so the state is 000 again.
  5. The cycle is 000 → 001 → 010 → 111 → 000: four states, and 011, 100, 101, 110 are never reached.

Answer: 4 states

Common misconception. Assuming the counter is a binary up-counter and answering 8. The feedback equations decide the cycle; a counter with a bad feedback network can lock into a short cycle, which is exactly why self-starting is checked in design.

Takeaway. Always simulate a counter from its flip-flop input equations. A cycle shorter than 2n means the counter is not self-starting.

DL-U4-S2-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about counters are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A 4-bit Johnson counter has eight states in its main cycle.
  2. A 4-bit ring counter has sixteen states in its main cycle.
  3. In an asynchronous ripple counter the flip-flops do not change at the same instant.
  4. A modulo-6 counter can be built from three flip-flops.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three families behave differently: a ripple counter clocks each stage from the previous one, a ring counter circulates a single pattern, and a Johnson counter feeds back the complement, which doubles the number of states.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. A Johnson counter with n stages has 2n states in its main cycle, so 4 stages give 8.
  2. (B) False. A 4-bit ring counter has only 4 states in its main cycle: 1000, 0100, 0010, 0001.
  3. (C) True. Each stage is clocked by the output of the stage before it, so the delays accumulate and the outputs settle one after another.
  4. (D) True. Three flip-flops give 8 states, which is enough for a modulo-6 count; two flip-flops give only 4, which is not.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) only

Common misconception. Marking (B) true because a 4-bit register has 24 = 16 codes. A ring counter never leaves the 4 codes with a single 1; the other codes form shorter cycles or are never entered.

Takeaway. Ring: n states. Johnson: 2n states. Ripple: all 2n states, but not simultaneously.

DL-U4-S2-Q21NATeasy

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Johnson counter passes through 2n states per cycle, so every output, including the last stage, repeats once every 2n clocks and runs at f / 2n.

Step by step.

  1. With n = 4, the main cycle holds 8 states.
  2. The last stage is 0 for four consecutive states and 1 for the next four, so its period is 8 clock cycles.
  3. Frequency 8 MHz / 8 = 1 MHz.
  4. In kHz that is 1000.

Answer: 1000 kHz

Common misconception. Dividing by 4, which is the ring-counter rule, and giving 2000 kHz. A Johnson counter divides by 2n, not by n.

Takeaway. Ring divides by n, Johnson by 2n. The extra state comes from feeding back the complement.

DL-U4C-Q01MCQeasy

A modulo-10 counter and a modulo-8 counter are cascaded so that the terminal count of the first counter clocks the second. What is the modulus of the combined counter?

  1. 40
  2. 80
  3. 18
  4. 64
Show answer & solution
